#10840. Beyond the Password Reset: Building Recovery Experiences That Protect Online Shopping

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An unfamiliar delivery address appears in a shopper’s account. Minutes later, an order confirmation arrives for something they never purchased. They try to sign in, but their password no longer works.

For the consumer, this is an urgent problem: stop the misuse, recover access, and understand what happened. For developers, it exposes a gap between building a login screen and building a service that remains useful when that login is compromised.

This article examines recovery from a business-to-consumer perspective: what an individual shopper needs, and how developers can translate those needs into safer interfaces and backend controls.

Account takeover and identity theft can overlap, but they are different problems.

Account takeover means someone has gained unauthorized control of an existing account. Identity theft involves misuse of someone’s personal information. Recovering access to one shopping account does not necessarily resolve misuse elsewhere.

Design the interface around questions a worried customer can answer:

A recovery page should explain the scope of help. “Recover your shopping account” is a specific promise. “Restore your identity” describes a much broader outcome that an ordinary retailer cannot deliver on its own.

Before building the workflow, list the consumer-visible assets involved: account access, addresses, order history, payment references, contact details, and support messages. Each needs its own review or remediation step.

A shopper who cannot sign in must still be able to locate help. Provide a public “My account may be compromised” entry point alongside ordinary password recovery.

Separate the two journeys. Someone who forgot a password usually needs a credential reset. Someone reporting suspicious activity may also need unauthorized changes reversed, orders investigated, and existing access revoked.

Avoid using a submitted email address as proof of ownership. A public report can initiate triage, but it should not expose order history or authorize sensitive changes.

OWASP’s Forgot Password Cheat Sheet recommends consistent responses for existing and nonexistent accounts, rate limiting, and securely generated reset tokens that expire and work only once.

If the details match an account, we’ll send instructions through an available recovery method.

The frontend message is only one part of that protection. Different response timing or API behavior can still reveal account existence.

After verifying a recovery factor, resist granting immediate access to every account feature.

A useful architecture gives the consumer a short-lived recovery session with narrowly defined permissions. For example, it might permit reviewing security settings while preventing purchases, data exports, or changes to payment information.

This type documents intent; it does not enforce security. The server must validate the session, its expiry, its association with the account, and the required permission on every request.

Check resource ownership separately. Permission to request an order review must never allow a consumer to submit another customer’s order identifier.

Do not treat an attacker’s recently changed email address as trustworthy recovery evidence. Review recovery options against previously established evidence and the account’s required level of assurance.

A new password is insufficient if an attacker still holds a valid session.

OWASP’s compromise-recovery guidance calls for reviewing recovery contacts and authentication methods, removing unauthorized changes, and invalidating existing sessions and outstanding recovery credentials after successful recovery.

For a consumer application, translate that into a visible checklist:

Use a durable workflow so a partial failure cannot silently appear as completion. Credential replacement and session revocation may span different systems; record pending work, retry failed operations safely, and show the consumer an accurate status.

JWT-based applications need particular care. Changing a password does not automatically invalidate an independently verifiable token. Choose an enforceable revocation strategy, such as checking a server-side session record or account security version on protected requests.

The OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet provides background on session lifecycle controls.

Recovery screens are often used on a phone, in a hurry, by someone who is uncertain about what happened.

Use short steps with clear outcomes: “Review recent changes,” “Secure your sign-in,” and “Check your orders.” Explain why information is requested before asking for it.

Use native form controls with visible labels. Associate field instructions and errors with their inputs, keep keyboard navigation predictable, and move focus deliberately when a step changes. SitePoint’s HTML Form Elements guide is useful background for building these controls.

A simple reporting form might begin with:

Provide an alternative when the consumer cannot access their usual inbox. That alternative needs its own verification process; support staff should not bypass security simply because a caller sounds convincing.

Once the immediate account problem is contained, a consumer may need help addressing misuse of personal information elsewhere.

Keep this guidance separate from the application’s own recovery controls. A retailer can investigate its orders and secure its accounts. It cannot directly correct records held by unrelated organizations.

For U.S. consumers, link to the FTC’s IdentityTheft.gov as a government resource. Offer country-appropriate resources when serving other regions.

Consumers considering paid assistance can also review an identity recovery service to understand the support available for broader restoration tasks. They should check eligibility, exclusions, and service terms before relying on it.

External assistance should remain optional. Restoring access to a customer’s account should not depend on purchasing another product.

Consumers need an understandable record of what happened. Offer a case summary with relevant timestamps, reported changes, affected orders, completed actions, and unresolved questions.

Separate customer-facing information from internal investigation details. A consumer may need to know that an address changed; they do not need raw authentication tokens or internal fraud-detection rules.

Treat recovery data as sensitive. Avoid recording passwords, recovery secrets, or full payment details in logs. Restrict staff access to case information and define retention periods appropriate to its purpose.

If evidence uploads are necessary, provide a secured upload route with access controls and a deletion policy. Do not ask customers to send sensitive documents to an ordinary support inbox by default.

A successful redirect is a weak definition of recovery.

After recovery, help consumers strengthen their sign-in. SitePoint’s Developer Guide to Implementing Passkeys offers a starting point for exploring passkey authentication.

A stronger sign-in method still needs a carefully designed fallback. Evaluate the recovery path whenever you change authentication.

For a shopper, recovery succeeds when unauthorized access stops, legitimate access returns, and the next steps are clear.

Developers can support that outcome by treating recovery as a complete product journey: limited permissions, enforceable revocation, accessible interfaces, accurate case status, and clear boundaries around external assistance.

The most useful question in a design review is simple: if this were your account, could you regain control and tell what still needs attention?

#10831. Top 5 3D Animation Software in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Choosing the best 3D animation software depends on your experience, budget, and creative goals. Some tools offer complete 3D modeling and animation workflows, while others specialize in professional character animation, visual effects, or AI-powered video creation. This guide compares five options and notes how each fits into web and game projects, so designers and developers can see which one suits their pipeline.

Blender is one of the strongest free options for anyone looking for comprehensive 3D animation software. This open-source application combines modeling, sculpting, rigging, animation, rendering, simulation, and compositing in one package.

Blender provides a broad production workflow without requiring users to pay for a standard commercial license. Its active community, learning resources, and add-on ecosystem make it accessible to independent creators. It is suitable for everything from simple animated scenes to complex short films and game assets.

For web and game developers: Blender ships with a glTF 2.0 importer and exporter, and glTF is the format libraries such as three.js load for 3D on the web. See the Blender manual’s glTF page for export options.

Its extensive interface and feature set can feel overwhelming to new users. Learning advanced rigging, lighting, materials, and rendering takes time, and some established studios may have specialized pipelines built around other applications.

Beginners, freelancers, indie game developers, students, and professionals who need a powerful 3D animation tool without a software subscription.

Autodesk Maya is a professional 3D application widely associated with character animation, rigging, film production, and game development. It offers detailed control over character movement and complex animation workflows, making it a strong choice for artists working in established production environments.

Maya provides sophisticated tools for animating characters and managing complex scenes. Its established role in many professional workflows makes it a valuable skill for artists pursuing careers in film, television, and game development. It also offers detailed control over animation timing, character motion, and rig behavior.

Maya generally requires a paid subscription, which can be expensive for hobbyists and independent creators. Its advanced interface also takes time to learn, particularly for users who have never worked with professional 3D software.

Professional animators, film and television artists, game studios, and users who need advanced character animation and rigging capabilities.

Cinema 4D is a 3D modeling, animation, and rendering application known for its motion graphics workflow. It is widely used for commercial visuals, broadcast graphics, product animations, and creative work that combines 3D elements with graphic design.

Cinema 4D is a strong option for designers who want to create polished motion graphics and branded animation. Its MoGraph workflow can make repetitive or complex movements easier to manage, and its integration with common design workflows is helpful for agencies and creative teams.

For web and product designers: Cinema 4D includes a glTF exporter, which helps when a designer needs to hand a 3D model to a developer for the web. Maxon documents the options on its glTF export settings page.

Cinema 4D is a commercial product, so ongoing licensing costs may be a concern. Although it can handle a wide range of 3D work, users focused on highly specialized character animation or procedural visual effects may prefer tools designed around those workflows.

Motion designers, advertising agencies, broadcast artists, product visualization specialists, and creators who regularly produce animated graphics.

Houdini, developed by SideFX, is a 3D software package known for procedural workflows and advanced visual effects. Rather than relying entirely on manually created steps, artists can build systems that generate and modify complex scenes, simulations, and effects.

Houdini offers exceptional flexibility for technical artists and visual-effects specialists. Procedural workflows make it possible to create reusable systems and adjust complex effects efficiently. Its capabilities are particularly valuable when a project needs detailed simulations or large numbers of interconnected elements.

For game developers: SideFX offers Houdini Engine plugins for Unity and Unreal Engine, which let procedural Houdini assets be used inside those engines. SideFX explains the plugins in its Houdini Engine FAQ.

Houdini has a steep learning curve, especially for beginners who are unfamiliar with node-based workflows. Building effective procedural systems requires patience and technical understanding. While free learning options are available, some commercial uses and advanced workflows require an appropriate paid license.

Visual-effects artists, technical directors, simulation specialists, and experienced 3D creators working on demanding film, television, or game projects.

MagicLight.AI is different from the four tools above. It is an AI-powered video creation platform rather than a traditional 3D application, so it produces finished animated videos, not editable 3D scenes, rigs, or game assets. It is included here for readers who want animated storytelling without building a 3D pipeline. It supports AI-generated characters, scene creation, storyboarding, and voice features.

MagicLight.AI can reduce the time and technical effort needed to create animated videos. It is particularly useful for content creators, educators, marketers, and storytellers who want to focus on the script and final message rather than mastering a complex 3D production pipeline.

MagicLight.AI is not a replacement for dedicated 3D software when you need precise control over mesh topology, character rigs, keyframes, physics, or custom 3D scenes. Creators who need editable 3D assets for games or studio production will need a conventional 3D application such as the ones above.

Content creators, educators, and marketers who want to produce animated videos efficiently without learning a traditional 3D animation workflow.

The right software is the one that supports your actual production needs. Before choosing a tool, consider the following factors.

If you want to model objects, build character rigs, and control animation precisely, start with a dedicated 3D application such as Blender or Maya. If your goal is to create animated storytelling videos from scripts or ideas with less manual production work, an AI video tool such as MagicLight.AI may be a more convenient option.

Blender is an excellent starting point if you need a complete 3D toolset without a standard license fee. Houdini also has a free Apprentice edition with restrictions. Maya and Cinema 4D are commercial applications, so compare their current plans against your expected usage before committing.

Different applications suit different workflows. Blender is versatile, Maya excels at character animation, Cinema 4D is well suited to motion graphics, and Houdini is especially powerful for procedural effects. MagicLight.AI is better suited to AI-assisted video production than to detailed, manually controlled 3D scene creation.

Beginners may find Blender’s extensive learning resources helpful, although its broad feature set takes time to master. Maya and Houdini demand more specialized skills for advanced work, while Cinema 4D is often considered by motion designers seeking a more direct design-oriented workflow. AI video tools can reduce the amount of technical setup required, but still require creative direction and review.

Think about where the finished animation will be used. A web project may need a lightweight glTF model. A game project may require exportable 3D assets, rig compatibility, and a game-engine workflow. A commercial motion graphic may depend on rendering and compositing integration. A social video or animated explainer may prioritize turnaround time, narration, subtitles, and scene continuity.

The best 3D animation software in 2026 depends on your budget, experience, and intended output. Blender is the strongest starting point for many users who want a comprehensive free 3D workflow. Autodesk Maya is a compelling option for professional character animation, Cinema 4D suits motion graphics and commercial visuals, and Houdini stands out for procedural effects and complex simulations. MagicLight.AI offers a different approach: it helps creators generate animated videos with AI rather than manually building a traditional 3D production pipeline.

Blender is one of the best free options because it includes modeling, rigging, animation, rendering, and other production tools in a single open-source application. It is suitable for beginners as well as experienced artists.

Yes. Free software can support professional-quality work when used effectively. Blender, for example, provides a comprehensive toolset for animation and rendering. The final result depends on the artist’s skills, project requirements, hardware, and production workflow.

Blender is a strong choice for beginners who want to learn traditional 3D modeling and animation without paying for a standard software license. Those who mainly want to create animated videos from stories or scripts may prefer exploring an AI video creation platform such as MagicLight.AI.

Blender and Cinema 4D can both export glTF, a format widely used for 3D on the web. Blender is free, so it is the easiest place to start.

#10821. How to Test Location-Aware APIs with FastAPI and PostGIS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Catch coordinate-order bugs, distance mistakes, boundary cases, and bad spatial assumptions before they reach production.

A location endpoint can return HTTP 200 and still be wrong by kilometers. That is the uncomfortable part of testing geospatial APIs: ordinary API checks tell you whether the request worked, not whether the geography is correct.

Spatial bugs can look harmless in code review. Latitude and longitude get reversed. A radius value is treated as degrees instead of meters. A query works near one city but behaves differently elsewhere because the geometry column uses the wrong type. The JSON is valid, the tests are green, and the map is still wrong.

In this tutorial, you will build a small FastAPI endpoint backed by PostGIS and then test the spatial behavior directly. The examples use deterministic fixtures positioned at known distances, so the tests verify geography rather than only response structure.

The API exposes one endpoint: GET /places/nearby. A client sends longitude, latitude, and a radius in meters. The service returns nearby places as GeoJSON features, ordered by distance.

The useful part is not the endpoint itself. The useful part is the test strategy. We will check coordinate validation, exact distance boundaries, GeoJSON coordinate order, and the database query that turns a radius into a spatial filter.

Use a geography column when the API is answering human-scale distance questions in meters. PostGIS can then calculate on the ellipsoid instead of treating longitude and latitude as flat Cartesian coordinates.

The endpoint below validates longitude, latitude, and radius with Pydantic, then sends a parameterized query to PostgreSQL. The SQL uses ST_DWithin to filter candidates and ST_Distance to return the measured distance.

The API accepts lon and lat as separate named parameters. That removes ambiguity at the HTTP boundary. GeoJSON still uses [longitude, latitude] when the response is created.

Hard-coding two real businesses and hoping their distance is approximately right makes a fragile test. A better approach is to create test points at exact distances from a fixed origin.

PostGIS can generate those fixtures with ST_Project. In the test database, create one point 500 meters away, one 950 meters away, and one 1,500 meters away. Now a 1,000-meter query has an unambiguous expected result: the first two belong in the response and the third does not.

This is the first test that actually proves something spatial. It is not checking that the endpoint returns a list. It is checking that the 1-kilometer contract means what the API says it means.

GeoJSON coordinates are longitude first, latitude second. Many developers naturally think latitude first because that is how coordinates are commonly spoken. A validation layer can catch the most obvious reversal before the database ever sees it.

Longitude must be between -180 and 180. Latitude must be between -90 and 90. That will not catch every reversal, because both numbers may sometimes fall inside both ranges, but it catches a large class of failures and gives clients a useful error instead of a misleading map.

A response can be syntactically valid JSON and still violate the GeoJSON contract. Assert the geometry type and the coordinate order. For a known query point, longitude should appear in element zero and latitude in element one.

Spatial boundaries deserve their own tests. A place at 999 meters should be included in a 1,000-meter search. A place at 1,001 meters should not. A place exactly at the boundary should behave consistently with the predicate you chose.

ST_DWithin includes features whose distance is less than or equal to the supplied threshold, so document that behavior and test it explicitly.

Unit tests are useful for request validation, but mocking the repository layer removes the most important part of this API: PostGIS itself. Keep a small integration-test database and run at least a few tests against the real extension.

That catches mistakes that mocks cannot: geography versus geometry behavior, SRID mismatches, spatial indexes, units, and changes to SQL functions. A location API is one of the cases where a cheap database integration test buys a lot of confidence.

Correctness is only half the story. A radius endpoint that scans every feature will work in development and become painful at production scale. Add a GiST index and inspect the query plan.

The exact plan depends on your data and PostgreSQL version, but you should see the spatial index participating in candidate filtering. Treat an accidental sequential scan on a large production table as a regression worth investigating.

For a real project, I would split the tests into two groups. Fast validation tests run on every commit. PostGIS integration tests run in CI with a temporary PostgreSQL/PostGIS service.

The CI gate should cover invalid coordinates, zero or negative radius values, known-distance fixtures, boundary behavior, GeoJSON output, and at least one query-plan or performance sanity check for larger datasets.

This is more work than asserting status_code == 200, but it tests the thing users actually care about: whether the API's answer is geographically true.

Location APIs fail in ways ordinary web tests are not designed to notice. The response can have the right schema, the database call can succeed, and every field can look reasonable while the location itself is wrong.

The fix is not a giant testing framework. Start with a few spatial invariants you can prove: known distances, valid coordinate ranges, correct GeoJSON order, deterministic boundary behavior, and real PostGIS integration tests. Those checks turn geography from an assumption into something your CI pipeline can verify.

#10816. 7 Checks Developers Should Make So AI Search Can Read Their Site

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Search results now include AI-written answers that cite pages, and people increasingly ask chat tools for recommendations instead of scrolling a list of links. For developers, this does not mean learning a new discipline. It means making sure the basics are in place so that crawlers and answer engines can reach, read and quote your pages.

Most problems are plain engineering mistakes: a blocked crawler, a page that renders nothing without JavaScript, or markup that disagrees with the visible text. This article covers seven checks, including one script you can run against your own robots.txt.

AI features in search do not need a special file or a special format. Google's documentation on AI features and your website states that a page needs to be indexed and eligible to appear in Search with a snippet, and that there are no additional technical requirements. It also says there is no need for new AI-specific files or special schema.org markup.

So start with the fundamentals. Check for accidental noindex tags after a deploy, confirm important pages return a 200 status, and make sure staging settings never reach production.

Answer engines read text. If your key information lives in images, canvas elements or content that only appears after a client-side script runs, a crawler may never see it. View the page source and the rendered HTML, and compare them. Product names, prices, definitions and steps should be present as real text.

Structured data helps systems understand a page, but only if it agrees with what visitors see. SitePoint's guide to JSON-LD for developers shows how to build markup that search and AI systems can parse. Generate it from the same data that renders the page, and validate it after every template change. Markup that contradicts the visible text is worse than no markup.

Your robots.txt decides which crawlers may fetch your pages. Some sites block AI crawlers deliberately, and others block them by accident through a broad rule. SitePoint's technical guide to working with a marketing agency lists robots.txt decisions about which crawlers may access the site among the checks developers should own.

This small function reads a robots.txt string and reports which common AI-related crawlers are blocked from the whole site:

Run it against your live robots.txt. If a crawler appears in the list and you did not intend that, fix the rule. Note that blocking a crawler is a legitimate choice. The goal is that it is a decision and not an accident.

Developers can check access and markup, but judging how a brand shows up in AI answers often needs someone who tracks that area full time. An AI search consultant such as Austin Heaton works on answer engine optimization.

Traffic from AI features in Google Search is included in the Search Console Performance report under the Web search type. Set up that report, tag your own campaigns with consistent UTM parameters, and watch referral traffic from chat tools in your analytics. You cannot control the answers, but you can see whether clicks arrive.

Most visibility problems appear after a deployment: a template change drops the structured data, a new rule blocks a crawler, or a redesign hides text behind a script. Add a small post-deploy test for the checks above. A failing robots.txt check or a missing JSON-LD block should stop the release before a crawler finds the problem.

Do I need an llms.txt file or special markup for AI search? Google's documentation says no new AI-specific files or special structured data are needed for its AI features. Standard indexing and crawling requirements apply.

Should I block AI crawlers? That depends on your goals. Blocking is a legitimate choice, but make it deliberately and review it regularly.

Where do I see AI search traffic? For Google, AI feature traffic is counted in the Search Console Performance report under the Web search type. Other tools show up as referrals in your analytics.

Making a site readable to AI search is mostly ordinary engineering: keep pages indexable, put key content in text, keep structured data consistent, decide crawler access on purpose, measure what you can and retest after every release. The robots.txt check above is small, but it catches one of the most common accidental blocks before it costs you visibility.

#10814. The Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard 2

The growing availability of LLMs supporting Arabic, both as monolingual and multilingual models, prompted the community to create dedicated Arabic language leaderboards. Previously, Arabic-focused leaderboards were typically confined to narrow benchmarks introduced by specific authors, often as demos for their work. In these cases, the authors would set up leaderboards to demonstrate how models performed on a particular task or dataset. Alternatively, other leaderboards required users to run evaluations on their own computing resources and then submit a JSON file containing their results for display.

While these approaches helped spark initial interest in Arabic benchmarking, they also introduced several challenges:

These limitations underscore the need for a more unified, accessible, and transparent benchmarking platform—one that not only enables but encourages genuine and reproducible experimentation for the entire Arabic NLP community. To address these issues, in May 2024, 2A2I, TII, and HuggingFace launched the first version of the Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard - OALL [1], featuring 14 benchmarks across a wide range of tasks including reading comprehension, sentiment analysis, and question answering among others.

In September 2024, a collaboration between SDAIA and the King Salman Global Academy for Arabic Language introduced the Balsam Index, which includes approximately 1,400 datasets with 50,000 questions covering 67 tasks, such as grammar correction, paraphrasing, cause-and-effect classification, and text comprehension ... etc.

Later that year, on December 5th, Inception and MBZUAI announced the AraGen Leaderboard, the first generative tasks leaderboard for Arabic, introducing the 3C3H evaluation metric, which uses dynamic evaluation cycles with private test, and provides a native-Arabic and culturally-aware generative tasks dataset, AraGen Bench, to assess LLMs across four main tasks.

And to end the year strong, on 19th of December 2024, Scale's Safety, Evaluations, and Alignment Lab (SEAL) published an Arabic leaderboard as part of their multilingual leaderboards. The benchmark empowering this leaderboard remains always private like all the other languages within their family of leaderboards, and relies on human-preference evaluation, using a dataset of 1,000 Arabic prompts designed to enhance chatbot interaction capabilities across complex and culturally nuanced conversations.

In less than 7 months after its launch, the first version of the Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard quickly became a vital platform for the Arabic AI community, attracting over 46,000 visitors and more than 2,000 visits in the past month (January 2025). The HuggingFace space received over 100 likes and 8 citations on Google Scholar. The community submitted more than 700 models, ranging from 1B to over 70B parameters. The submitted models originate from more than 180 unique organizations making it one of the most active LLM evaluation leaderboards. Since its launch, the leaderboard sparked numerous engaging discussions across social media, HuggingFace, Reddit, making it the most prominent Arabic leaderboard to date.

As depicted in Figure 1, among the ~700 models submitted to the initial version of the leaderboard, the majority are chat and finetuned models, comprising over 70%, whereas pretrained models constitute only 11%. In terms of model size, more than 50% of the models are smaller than 7B parameters.

Compared to leaderboards for other languages and as shown in Figure 2, the Open Arabic LLM leaderboard stands out as one of the most active, following closely behind the Korean, Polish, and Portuguese leaderboards, all within less than a year of its launch. Considering that Arabic is one of the most spoken languages globally, yet has relatively limited content available on the internet, these figures carry even greater significance compared to other languages.

Recent discussions within the community, including critiques of the Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard (OALL) and similar initiatives, have highlighted key shortcomings in current benchmarking practices [2]. Many researchers, developers, and language enthusiasts have emphasized the need for more direct evaluations of Arabic-specific tasks, increased transparency in how benchmarks are created, and the inclusion of more diverse datasets that reflect the breadth of Arabic dialects, domains, and real-world applications. These insights have played a central role in shaping the updated leaderboard.

The Arabic language presents unique challenges and characteristics that require specialized evaluation beyond what general NLP tasks can capture. These include intricate grammar, rich and complex morphology, the diversity of spoken dialects, and culturally nuanced safety-related considerations. A leaderboard that addresses these factors can provide a clearer picture of how well models perform in real-world Arabic language contexts.

In the first iteration of OALL, a large portion of datasets and tasks originated from non-Arabic-speaking contexts. When adapted to Arabic, these tasks often failed to reflect real-world use cases or meet the practical needs of Arabic-speaking communities. Many tasks were direct translations from English, which frequently introduced linguistic and contextual mismatches. This approach overlooked Arabic’s unique morphological and syntactic complexities, making the tasks less effective in measuring true language understanding and modeling capabilities.

Additionally, some benchmarks from the first version of OALL became less effective over time as models achieved near-perfect scores, limiting their ability to differentiate incremental improvements. In response, the new leaderboard replaces these saturated benchmarks, introducing a more relevant and up-to-date suite of evaluation tasks.

To address these gaps, the new leaderboard incorporates tasks that are natively developed in Arabic. These tasks are designed to capture the language’s distinctive features—such as its rich morphology, subtle syntax, and context-specific usage—elements that are often lost in translation-based benchmarks. This shift ensures that evaluations are more authentic and better aligned with the realities of Arabic language use.

Furthermore, we identified a silent bug in one of the main tasks, AlGhafa, which inadvertently impacted model rankings. The issue stemmed from a mismatch in how answer choices were checked—rather than verifying their indices, the task evaluated responses based on the choices themselves. While this was not entirely incorrect, it affected small/weak models disproportionately. Some models experienced score drops of up to 20 points, while stronger models remained relatively unaffected. This issue compromised the consistency, fairness and uniformity of the evaluations.

In reforming the leaderboard, we follow two guiding principles: remove saturated and machine translated tasks, due to inherent lower quality and possible cultural bias, and add newly available high quality native or human curated benchmarks to increase the coverage of the evaluation.

From the first version of the Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard (OALL), we keep the following benchmark datasets:

We enrich the leaderboard by adding the following datasets, released in the past year:

Finally, we introduce the ALRAGE benchmark: Arabic Language Retrieval Augmented Generation Evaluation. It introduces a comprehensive framework for evaluating Large Language Models' retrieval-augmented generation capabilities in Arabic. Built upon a meticulously curated dataset sourced from 40 Arabic books spanning diverse topics, from Arts & Literature to Technology & Innovation, the benchmark was created using meta-llama/Meta-Llama-3.1-70B for synthetic generation and validated by native Arabic speakers with a community sprint with Argilla. The dataset structure includes questions, ground-truth answers, candidate contexts retrieved through the BAAI/bge-m3 embedding model, and target candidate indices, all designed to authentically simulate real-world RAG scenarios in Arabic.

The innovative aspect of ALRAGE lies in its evaluation methodology, which implements a LLM-as-judge metric within the lighteval framework. Using Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct as the judge model, the system evaluates generated responses through a structured Arabic prompt that compares the model's output against gold answers. The evaluation employs a nuanced 0-10 scoring rubric that assesses answer accuracy, relevance, and quality, with scores normalized to a 0-1 range for standardization. This technical implementation, manifested through a custom JudgeMetricWrapper class, provides a rigorous, reproducible method for evaluating Arabic language generation while maintaining sensitivity to Arabic linguistic nuances, effectively addressing the critical need for sophisticated evaluation metrics in Arabic NLP.

Table 1 summarizes the datasets kept from the first version of the leaderboard as well as the new datasets introduced in this second version.

Besides adding and removing datasets, we fixed multiple issues related to the UI and its filters, and we also introduced chat templates. In terms of user submissions, now the number of submissions is limited to 5 per organization per week. This limitation is meant to limit the usage of the leaderboard and give the chance to varied organizations to have their models evaluated. NOTE that for the models submitted by OALL's team to v2, if chat template is found in the config, it is used for the evaluation. Otherwise, chat template is disabled.

To assess the impact of the second iteration of the Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard, we conducted a series of statistical comparisons between the two versions.

Figure 3 displays the performance scores across six benchmarks for Versions 1 and 2. Notably, ACVA and Toxigen demonstrate saturation effects at various model sizes. Alghafa in Version 1 exhibits lower saturation, which we hypothesize is due to the inclusion of both native and translated Arabic benchmarks. In contrast, the models' performance for AraTrust, ALRAGE, and Alghafa form v2 is more dispersed with respect to model size.

To examine the correlation between OALL and other Arabic LLM leaderboards, we compared the relative rankings of five open Arabic LLMs: google/gemma-2-27b-it, CohereForAI/aya-23-35B, CohereForAI/aya-expanse-32b, inceptionai/jais-adapted-70b-chat, and meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct, across three leaderboards: OALL v2, SEAL Arabic, and AraGen. As illustrated in Figure 4, a correlation between the leaderboards is notable, with the Llama3.3-70-instruct model ranking first on both OALL v2 and AraGen, and third on SEAL. *As a clarification, AraGen currently only features the scores for inceptionai/jais-adapted-70b-chat, and the Arabic SEAL leaderboard only includes Jais Adapted 70B, so presumably the pretrained model. As we could not fully solve this discrepancy, we decided to evaluate inceptionai/jais-adapted-70b-chat on OALL v2 for this comparison.

To further explore the differences between the two versions of OALL, we present in Figure 5 the top models across two categories: pretrained and chat. For models submitted to OALL v1, Qwen2.5 establishes itself as a strong baseline for Arabic in all categories, particularly for pretrained models. In OALL v2, Qwen models also dominate the pretrained models category, however the Qwen/Qwen2-72B model surpasses Qwen/Qwen2.5-72B as the best pretrained/continually pretrained model, and Llama3.3-70B-instruct emerges as the leader in all categories, surpassing calme-2.1-qwen2.5-72b in performance. Overall, some model rankings have shifted in v2, while others have remained consistent. We attribute these changes to two key factors: first, the robustness of models with respect to Arabic-native benchmarks, safety, and trustworthiness; and second, the evaluation of over 700 models in OALL v1 compared to 80 models in v2, including a few recent models that might not be present in v1. We anticipate that the community will contribute to expanding the leaderboard following its release.

Finally, we analyzed the average scores on OALL v1 and v2 for two model families: AceGPT and Jais. As depicted in Figure 6, the trend is consistent across both versions: larger models tend to achieve higher average scores, with the exception of inceptionai/jais-family-30b-8k, that surpasses the larger inceptionai/jais-adapted-70b model on OALL v2. Overall, the average scores in v2 are higher than in v1, except for the 7B models in both families. We hypothesize that this discrepancy is due to the lower performance of smaller models on ALRAGE, as it is a generative task, which typically favors larger models.

In this blog post, we introduced the second version of the Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard. We analyzed the existing Arabic leaderboards as well as the first version of the OALL, remarking issues such as the saturation of specific benchmarks that were removed in this second iteration. We also removed machine-translated benchmarks and retained only the Arabic native and human-translated benchmarks. Finally, we introduced new benchmarks such as Aratrust, MadinaQA, native MMLU, human translated MMLU (MMLU-HT) and ALRAGE. Our goal is to offer the community an objective evaluation of Arabic LLMs, aiding in the understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of each submitted model.

Looking ahead, we hope to see the release of additional Arabic benchmarks, particularly in areas such as mathematics, reasoning, hallucination and both general and domain-specific benchmarks.

The authors would like to thank Mohamed bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence (MBZUAI) for providing some of the new native benchmarks we are using in this version, including the new MMLU-HT dataset. We also extend our gratitude to TII for their generous sponsorship of the inference hardware needed for the evaluation backend. We also thank our friends at Hugging Face for their continuous support and being always 🤗 whenever needed. Thanks to all people focusing on evaluation and leaderboards for their languages and tasks. Lastly, we thank the community for their engagement and valuable feedback on the first version of the OALL. Looking forward to seeing many models on the leaderboard 🚀.

#10813. Open R1: Update #2

We are now two weeks into the Open R1 project which aims to reconstruct the missing pieces of DeepSeek R1—specifically, the training pipeline and synthetic data.

In this post, we are happy to share the construction of OpenR1-Math-220k: our first large-scale dataset for mathematical reasoning!

We also take a look at some exciting developments from the community towards curating small, high-quality datasets for fine-tuning, along with insights into how to control the length of the chain-of-thought from reasoning models at both train-time and inference-time.

One of the key advantages of DeepSeek R1 is its ability to transfer advanced reasoning capabilities to smaller models through distillation. The DeepSeek team demonstrated this by generating 600k reasoning traces and fine-tuning a series of Qwen and Llama models, showing that direct distillation from R1 can achieve competitive reasoning performance without reinforcement learning. Notably, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B achieved 55.5% on AIME 2024, surpassing larger models like QwQ-32B-Preview.

However, the reasoning traces used for distillation have not been released publicly, prompting the community to independently recreate similar datasets. So far, multiple open datasets have been released by the community, including OpenThoughts-114k, Bespoke-Stratos-17k, Dolphin-R1, and LIMO.

🐳  Introducing OpenR1-Math-220k, a large-scale math reasoning dataset generated locally on 512 H100s, with multiple answers per problem. To create OpenR1-Math-220k, we collaborated with Numina who have developed a brand new version of their popular NuminaMath-CoT dataset.

What’s new in OpenR1 dataset compared to existing datasets:

By demonstrating scalable, high-quality reasoning data generation, we hope this pipeline can be extended beyond math to domains like code generation.

To build OpenR1-220k, we prompt DeepSeek R1 to generate solutions for 400k problems from NuminaMath 1.5. We follow the model card’s recommended parameters and prepend the following instruction to the user prompt:

"Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}."

We set a 16k token limit per generation, as our analysis showed that only 75% of problems could be solved in under 8k tokens, and most of the remaining problems required the full 16k tokens. Initially, we used vLLM for inference, achieving a throughput of 15 generations per hour per H100, and shared our generation scripts in previous updates and on the OpenR1 repo. Recently, we started experimenting with SGLang and we were able to generate 25 solutions per hour per H100 (almost 2x speedup!), enabling us to generate 300k problem solutions per day on 512 H100s. This allowed us to produce 800k reasoning traces in just a few days.

We generate two solutions per problem—and in some cases, four—to provide flexibility in filtering and training. This approach allows for rejection sampling, similar to DeepSeek R1’s methodology, and also makes the dataset suitable for preference optimisation methods like DPO.

The scripts for the data generation are available here: https://github.com/huggingface/open-r1/tree/main/slurm

The unfiltered dataset is available here: https://huggingface.co/datasets/open-r1/OpenR1-Math-Raw

To retain only high-quality, correct reasoning traces, we leverage Math Verify, a robust mathematical expression evaluation system designed to assess LLM-generated answers. We extract the final answers from model generations and compare them against ground truth answers in the dataset.

We find that 55% of problems have at least one correct answer. However, some ground truth answers in NuminaMath 1.5 were empty or not in a verifiable format, making automatic validation challenging. While we have improved Math-Verify to more accurately handle these more uncommon output formats (see Math-Verify improvements below), we also explored an alternative method to recover valid solutions from rejected samples: using Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct as a judge on a subset of rejected problems. Before running this verification step, we filter out samples that are incomplete or that contain an empty ground truth answer, ensuring that only well-formed responses with a clearly boxed final answer are considered. This process successfully retrieves 28,000 of previously rejected problems.

We prompt Llama3.3-70B-Instruct as follows:

By combining rule-based verification (Math Verify) with LLM-based evaluation, we improve dataset quality while maintaining scale. The final dataset consists of 220k problems with verified reasoning traces, making it a valuable resource for training reasoning models. Providing multiple solutions per problem gives the community flexibility to filter for better generations and apply more targeted refinements based on NuminaMath data sources and problem types.

For rows with multiple correct answers, we also tried applying a Reward Model (RM) as a final filter to select the best response. For each row with multiple correct generations by R1, we extracted the final answer by removing the thinking tokens (…), and then pass the problem + the extracted answer to Qwen/Qwen2.5-Math-RM-72B served using vLLM to get an score. Using these scores, we built a ranking for each row containing more than one correct response. The top-1 correct generations were selected and included in the training dataset, but sadly the training ablations showed that this approach doesn’t help to improve model performance with respect to selecting one random correct generation. A possible improvement could be to include the reasoning trace rather than just the final answer when scoring with the RM.

We fine-tune Qwen2.5-Math-Instruct for 3 epochs on the default split of the dataset using a learning rate of 5e-5. To extend the context length from 4k to 32k, we increase RoPE frequency to 300k. The training follows a linear learning rate schedule with a 10% warmup phase. The table below compares the performance of OpenR1-Qwen-7B to DeepSeek-Distill-Qwen-7B and OpenThinker-7B using lighteval.

This dataset represents an initial version, providing a foundation for further refinement. The community can explore additional filtering strategies to improve performance, such as rejection sampling, which was used in DeepSeek R1 to enhance quality.

We identified several failure cases in Math-Verify during our inspection of the verification results. To address these issues, we implemented significant improvements and fixes. We strongly recommend updating to the latest version (0.5.2) to benefit from these enhancements:

The following is the summary of the most important improvements:

This week saw the community explore GRPO from many different angles, while multiple research labs have shown that only ~1000 high quality training samples may be sufficient to elicit reasoning in existing open models.

The first part of the AIME 2025 was released this week, which consists of 15 difficult math problems that are used to train high school students for the International Math Olympiad. In the past year, AIME 2024 has stood as the main benchmark to probe the mathematical capabilities of LLMs and the community was excited to see how well models performs on a new set of unseen problems:

An interesting new research paper shows that by using a recurrent language model, it is possible to scale test-time compute by implicitly reasoning in latent space. This resembles Meta’s Coconut work to train language models in latent space, but now adapted to reasoning tasks. The advantage of these methods is that they are far more compute efficient: by exploring the latent, one does not need to generate huge amounts of “thinking” tokens to obtain high performance.

While DeepSeek R1 leveraged 600k reasoning traces for distillation, recent work suggests that complex reasoning can emerge in language models not through massive-scale training, but through a small number of carefully curated samples.

One example of this approach is the s1K dataset. It consists of 1,000 carefully selected math questions with distilled reasoning traces from Gemini Flash. The selection approach focuses on difficulty, diversity, and quality. The authors fine-tune Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct on s1K and manage to exceed OpenAI’s o1-preview on competition math benchmarks by up to 27%.

Another dataset, LIMO, pushes this idea further, achieving strong performance on AIME and MATH benchmarks using only 817 training samples.  The authors hypothesize that when a model has already acquired extensive domain knowledge during pre-training, only a small number of well-structured examples may be needed to unlock advanced reasoning capabilities.

One important ingredient allowing the fine-tuned Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct model from s1K to reach such strong performance is budget forcing, a test-time compute technique that either extends or truncates reasoning by appending “Wait” or an end-of-thinking token delimiter to the model’s generation, respectively. This tool allowed the authors to vary thinking time and conclude that their model exhibits test-time scaling: as thinking time increases, so does accuracy on different math benchmarks.

Similarly, Demystifying Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning in LLMs (Yeo et al.) also studies the effect of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) length on model performance. They introduce the Cosine Reward — a novel reward function that they use to incentivize shorter CoTs for correct generations and longer CoTs for wrong generations — which stabilizes RL training, particularly when the model has relatively limited max context size and average response length could explode. Repetition penalty is also employed when the model starts to show signs of reward hacking on hard questions, by artificially increasing CoT length through repetition instead of attempting to solve the problem.

Now that GRPO is humming in TRL, we are running an extensive set of experiments to understand which hyperparameters and reward functions have the greatest impact on training. You can follow our progress in the community tab and will write up our findings in the next update!

If you want to contribute check out the open-r1 repository on GitHub or follow the Hugging Face open-r1 org.

#10812. Build awesome datasets for video generation

(This post was authored by hlky and Sayak)

Tooling for image generation datasets is well established, with img2dataset being a fundamental tool used for large scale dataset preparation, and complemented with various community guides, scripts and UIs that cover smaller scale initiatives.

Our ambition is to make tooling for video generation datasets equally established, by creating open video dataset scripts suited for small scale, and leveraging video2dataset for large scale use cases.

“If I have seen further it is by standing on the shoulders of giants”

In this post, we provide an overview of the tooling we are developing to make it easy for the community to build their own datasets for fine-tuning video generation models. If you cannot wait to get started already, we welcome you to check out the codebase here.

Typically, video generation is conditioned on natural language text prompts such as: "A cat walks on the grass, realistic style". Then in a video, there are a number of qualitative aspects for controllability and filtering, like so:

Video generation models are only as good as the data they are trained on. Therefore, these aspects become crucial when curating the datasets for training/fine-tuning.

Our 3 stage pipeline draws inspiration from works like Stable Video Diffusion, LTX-Video, and their data pipelines.

Like video2dataset we opt to use yt-dlp for downloading videos.

We create a script Video to Scenes to split long videos into short clips.

Florence-2 microsoft/Florence-2-large to run Florence-2 tasks , , and on extracted frames. This provides different captions, object recognition and OCR that can be used for filtering in various ways.

We can bring in any other captioner in this regard. We can also caption the entire video (e.g., with a model like Qwen2.5) as opposed to captioning individual frames.

In the dataset for the model finetrainers/crush-smol-v0, we opted for captions from Qwen2VL and we filtered on pwatermark < 0.1 and aesthetic > 5.5. This highly restrictive filtering resulted in 47 videos out of 1493 total.

Let's review the example frames from pwatermark -

Two with text have scores of 0.69 and 0.61

The "toy car with a bunch of mice in it" scores 0.60 then 0.17 as the toy car is crushed.

All example frames were filtered by pwatermark < 0.1. pwatermark is effective at detecting text/watermarks however the score gives no indication whether it is a text overlay or a toy car's license plate. Our filtering required all scores to be below the threshold, an average across frames would be a more effective strategy for pwatermark with a threshold of around 0.2 - 0.3.

Let's review the example frames from aesthetic scores -

The pink castle initially scores 5.5 then 4.44 as it is crushed

The action figure scores lower at 4.99 dropping to 4.84 as it is crushed.

In our filtering we required all scores to be below the threshold, in this case using the aesthetic score from the first frame only would be a more effective strategy.

If we review finetrainers/crush-smol we can notice that many of the objects being crushed are round or rectangular and colorful which is similar to our findings in the example frames. Aesthetic scores can be useful yet have a bias that will potentially filter out good data when used with extreme thresholds like > 5.5. It may be more effective as a filter for bad content than good with a minimum threshold of around 4.25 - 4.5.

Here we provide some visual examples for each filter as well as the captions from Florence-2.

We have created various datasets with the tooling in an attempt to generate cool video effects, similar to the Pika Effects:

We then used these datasets to fine-tune the CogVideoX-5B model using finetrainers. Below is an example output from finetrainers/crush-smol-v0:

We hope this tooling gives you a headstart to create small and high-quality video datasets for your own custom applications. We will continue to add more useful filters to the repository, so, please keep an eye out. Your contributions are also more than welcome 🤗

#10811. Stop Overpaying for App Builders: How to Build Premium Websites and Web Apps with AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a professional website or web application no longer requires a large development budget. AI coding agents have changed how developers approach design, development, and deployment. Yet many founders and freelancers still assume they need to pay for a dedicated AI app builder before they can create something polished.

Visual app builders such as Lovable and Bolt can help users turn ideas into working prototypes quickly. Their convenience is valuable, especially for beginners. However, subscriptions, usage limits, and additional services can increase the cost of building and maintaining a project.

There is another approach: use AI coding agents with a development stack you control.

I discovered this firsthand when I moved beyond WordPress development and built a CRM from scratch.

Instead of relying on a dedicated app builder, I used Claude, Next.js, Firebase Firestore, Tailwind CSS, Git, and Vercel.

The experience changed how I think about the cost of building software.

App builders reduce the technical barriers to software development. You describe what you want, and the platform generates interfaces, application logic, and sometimes database integrations.

The problem is not the tools themselves. It is assuming that a paid app builder is the only practical way to build a professional product.

Consider what happens when a project grows. You may need more iterations, additional integrations, more control over the generated code, or a different deployment setup. Depending on the platform and plan, these requirements can introduce additional costs or constraints.

There is also a difference between creating a prototype and building a maintainable application. Generating a dashboard is relatively straightforward. Ensuring that its permissions, database operations, error handling, and security work correctly requires additional engineering.

Paying for an app builder does not automatically solve these problems.

Before subscribing to another platform, ask a more useful question: Am I paying for a capability I genuinely need, or am I paying for a workflow I could manage directly?

I have designed more than 100 websites and helped clients implement over 12 CRM and ERP systems. My experience gave me a strong understanding of website design, business workflows, and the practical problems software needs to solve.

However, configuring existing platforms is different from developing an application from scratch.

When I decided to build a CRM, I wanted to learn the entire process, from writing code to managing data and deploying the application. I also wanted to avoid spending money on a dedicated app-builder subscription when I could use AI coding tools with a more flexible development environment.

I chose Claude as my AI coding agent and used a stack built around Next.js, Firebase Firestore, Tailwind CSS, Git, and Vercel.

This approach helped me move from requirements to implementation without depending on a single visual app-building platform. I also saved more than $1,000 in potential app-builder costs and related expenses compared with the approach I had considered.

That figure reflects my own project and spending decisions. It is not a guarantee that every developer will save the same amount.

More importantly, I gained practical experience with API integrations, application security, performance, and user experience. Those lessons became part of my technical portfolio.

You do not need to reproduce my exact stack for every project. The important idea is to choose tools that work together and give you an appropriate level of control.

An AI coding agent can help generate components, implement features, explain unfamiliar code, and investigate errors.

I used Claude to accelerate implementation and work through development challenges. Instead of asking it to build the entire application in one prompt, I worked through smaller tasks, reviewed the output, and refined the results.

The developer still needs to understand the requirements and verify the generated code. AI can accelerate development, but it cannot guarantee correctness.

Next.js provided the framework for building the web application. It supports structured development, routing, and different approaches to rendering and application logic.

For developers who want more control than a visual builder provides, working directly with a framework also makes it easier to understand how the application is organized.

Firestore provided the database layer for my project. Its document-based data model and integration with the Firebase ecosystem made it a practical choice for the application.

However, using a managed database does not eliminate the need to design data structures carefully, configure access rules, and evaluate query performance as usage grows.

A premium-looking interface depends on more than colors and typography. Spacing, consistency, responsiveness, accessibility, and clear user flows all influence the result.

Tailwind CSS helped me build and refine the interface using utility classes, making iterative design changes easier to manage.

Git allowed me to track changes, review revisions, and recover from problematic updates. Vercel provided the deployment environment and a convenient workflow for connecting releases to the codebase.

Together, these tools supported a development process that was not tied to a dedicated app-builder subscription.

A low-cost stack is only useful if the development process is disciplined.

Start by defining the application's purpose, primary users, and essential features. Then create a simple design system covering typography, spacing, colors, buttons, forms, and navigation.

Build one feature at a time. Ask the AI agent to explain its implementation, review important changes, and test each feature before expanding the application.

For a website, pay attention to responsive layouts, accessibility, loading speed, and search engine requirements. For a web application, also test authentication, permissions, database operations, and error handling.

Finally, deploy early enough to test the application in a realistic environment. Keep the code in version control and make sure you understand how to update, maintain, and recover the application.

These steps matter more than choosing whichever AI tool is currently receiving the most attention.

No. App builders can be excellent choices when speed, simplicity, and minimal setup matter most.

A founder validating an idea may benefit from launching a prototype quickly. A nontechnical user may prefer a managed platform over maintaining a codebase. A team may also find that a paid subscription costs less than the time required to manage its own development environment.

The right decision depends on the project.

If you need extensive customization, want direct control over your code, or expect to develop the application further, an AI coding agent combined with a conventional development stack may offer greater flexibility.

Compare the total cost of ownership rather than the subscription price alone. Include development time, hosting, database usage, API costs, security, maintenance, and the effort required to change platforms later.

AI has made software development more accessible, but accessibility does not mean every project needs another subscription.

My experience building a CRM taught me that a coding agent, a well-chosen technology stack, and a structured development process can be a practical alternative to a dedicated app builder.

The goal should not be to avoid spending money at all costs. It should be to spend where the investment creates genuine value.

Before paying for another tool, explore what you can accomplish with AI-assisted coding and tools you control. You may discover that the biggest limitation is not your budget, but your willingness to learn a different way of building.

Build with the tools that fit your project—not simply the tools that make the first demo easiest.

#10810. From Chunks to Blocks: Accelerating Uploads and Downloads on the Hub

Content-defined chunking (CDC) plays a central role in enabling deduplication within a Xet-backed repository. The idea is straightforward: break each file’s data into chunks, store only unique ones, reap the benefits.

In practice, it's more complex. If we focused solely on maximizing deduplication, the design would call for the smallest possible chunk size. By doing that, we’d create significant overheads for the infrastructure and the builders on the Hub.

On Hugging Face's Xet team, we're bringing CDC from theory to production to deliver faster uploads and downloads to AI builders (by a factor of 2-3x in some cases). Our guiding principle is simple: enable rapid experimentation and collaboration for teams building and iterating on models and datasets. This means focusing on more than just deduplication; we’re optimizing how data moves across the network, how it’s stored, and the entire development experience.

Imagine uploading a 200GB repository to the Hub. Today, there are a number of ways to do this, but all use a file-centric approach. To bring faster file transfers to the Hub, we've open-sourced xet-core and hf_xet, an integration with huggingface_hub which uses a chunk-based approach written in Rust.

If you consider a 200GB repository with unique chunks, that's 3 million entries (at ~64KB per chunk) in the content-addressed store (CAS) backing all repositories. If a new version of a model is uploaded or a branch in the repository is created with different data, more unique chunks are added, driving up the entries in the CAS.

With nearly 45PB across 2 million model, dataset, and space repositories on the Hub, a purely chunk-based approach could incur 690 billion chunks. Managing this volume of content using only chunks is simply not viable due to:

In short, network requests balloon, databases struggle to manage the metadata, and the cost of orchestrating each chunk skyrockets all while you wait for your files to transfer.

These challenges lead to a key realization:

Deduplication is a performance optimization, not the final goal.

The final goal is to improve the experience of builders iterating and collaborating on models and datasets. The system components from the client to the storage layer do not need to guarantee deduplication. Instead, they leverage deduplication as one tool among many to aid in this.

By loosening the deduplication constraint, we naturally arrive at a second design principle:

Avoid communication or storage strategies that scale 1:1 with the number of chunks.

What does this mean? We scale with aggregation.

Aggregation takes chunks and groups them, referencing them intelligently in ways that provide clever (and practical) benefits:

Together, blocks and shards unlock significant benefits. However, when someone uploads a new file, how do we know if a chunk has been uploaded before so we can eliminate an unnecessary request? Performing a network query for every chunk is not scalable and goes against the “no 1:1” principle we mentioned above.

The solution is key chunks which are a 0.1% subset of all chunks selected with a simple modulo condition based on the chunk hash. We provide a global index over these key chunks and the shards they are found in, so that when the chunk is queried, the related shard is returned to provide local deduplication. This allows us to leverage the principles of spatial locality. If a key chunk is referenced in a shard, it’s likely that other similar chunk references are available in the same shard. This further improves deduplication and reduces network and database requests.

The Hub currently stores over 3.5PB of .gguf files, most of which are quantized versions of other models on the Hub. Quantized models represent an interesting opportunity for deduplication due to the nature of quantization where values are restricted to a smaller integer range and scaled. This restricts the range of values in the weight matrices, naturally leading to more repetition. Additionally, many repositories of quantized models store multiple different variants (e.g., Q4_K, Q3_K, Q5_K) with a great deal of overlap.

A good example of this in practice is bartowski/gemma-2-9b-it-GGUF which contains 29 quantizations of google/gemma-2-9b-it totalling 191GB. To upload, we use hf_xet integrated with huggingface_hub to perform chunk-level deduplication locally then aggregate and store data at the block level.

Once uploaded, we can start to see some cool patterns! We’ve included a visualization that shows the deduplication ratio for each block. The darker the block, the more frequently parts of it are referenced across model versions. If you go to the Space hosting this visualization, hovering over any heatmap cell highlights all references to the block in orange across all models while clicking on a cell will select all other files that share blocks:

A single block of deduplication might only represent a few MB of savings, but as you can see there are many overlapping blocks! With this many blocks that quickly adds up. Instead of uploading 191GB, the Xet-backed version of the gemma-2-9b-it-GGUF repository stores 1515 unique blocks for a total of approximately 97GB to our test CAS environment (a savings of ~94GB).

While the storage improvements are significant, the real benefit is what this means for contributors to the Hub. At 50MB/s, the deduplication optimizations amount to a four hour difference in upload time; a speedup of nearly 2x:

Similarly, local chunk caching significantly speeds up downloads. If a file is changed or a new quantization is added that has significant overlap with the local chunk cache, you won’t have to re-download any chunks that are unchanged. This contrasts to the file-based approach where the entirety of the new or updated file must be downloaded.

Taken together, this demonstrates how local chunk-level deduplication paired with block-level aggregation dramatically streamlines not just storage, but developing on the Hub. By providing this level of efficiency in file transfers, AI builders can move faster, iterate quickly, and worry less about hitting infrastructure bottlenecks. For anyone pushing large files to the Hub (whether you're pushing a new model quantization or an updated version of a training set) this helps you shift focus to building and sharing, rather than waiting and troubleshooting.

We’re fast at work, rolling out the first Xet-backed repositories in the coming weeks and months! As we do that, we will be releasing more updates to bring these speeds to every builder on the Hub to make file transfers feel invisible.

Follow us on the Hub to learn more about our progress!

A view of dedupe from quants in bartowski/gemma-2-9b-it-GGUF

Nice to see that Xet backed repos are being rolled out on the Hub.

How can we find the chunk content using chunk hash? The CAS system only stores "block_hash -> block_content", Where does the map of chunk to block?

what does the shards store? Is it "file_name, shard_id, chunk_hash, block_hash"?

How can we find the chunk content using chunk hash?

Chunk hash is calculated via content-defined chunking (CDC), which means that if two chunks have the same content they will share the same hash. CDC removes the need to store the mapping between chunk hash -> chunk content because we know if two chunks share the same hash, they will have identical content.

The CAS system only stores "block_hash -> block_content", Where does the map of chunk to block?

This is explained in the "key chunks" section in the blog post above. Essentially we only store a tiny subset of chunk -> block by leveraging spatial locality in the file. Trying to store every mapping of chunk -> block can get impractical very quickly.

what does the shards store? Is it "file_name, shard_id, chunk_hash, block_hash"

You can think of the shards as storing mappings between file (identified via file hash) to list of chunks that make up the file.

I hope this help explains our underlying tech better!

If we don't store every chunk hash to block hash, how can we download a model file. For example if a model does not have a key chunk, the shard info only contains "file hash -> list of chunks", the CAS only has "block hash -> block content", how can we know which block is needed ?

For example if a model does not have a key chunk

Two points of clarification might be in order concerning the above point:

If we don't store every chunk hash to block hash, how can we download a model file

When downloading a model file, a request is made to our services with the file hash. This is mapped to a list of block subranges. Logically these are chunks, but by storing the offsets we save on the metadata storage and ultimately many offsets will share boundaries inside a block (allowing us to group them together in a response) providing benefits when sending the content back to the client.
